Given this uncertainty, as well as the inherent vulnerabilities of civilians, I would strongly bet on the potential for scientific progress to deliver yet-greater recipes for ruin.&nbsp;</p><h2>Conclusions and Research Recommendations</h2><p>The plan to harden society against the deluge of AI-enabled scientific progress is a kind of <a href="https://michaelnotebook.com/vwh/index.html#fnref7:~:text=Techno%2Dcapitalism%20and,the%20technological%20class.">technological solutionism</a>: the idea that the solution to (offensive) technology is more (defensive) technology. Rather than bite the bullet on a tradeoff between concentration of power and security, advocates of hardening often hope to dissolve the tradeoff with better technology, <a href="https://helentoner.substack.com/p/dynamism-vs-stasis#:~:text=A%20common%20reaction,those%20as%20black.">implicitly dismissing the possibility</a> that there might not actually be workable technological solutions to this problem.</p><p>I do not consider it a remote possibility. For the reasons described, I think it is basically a feature of reality that cheap, ultra-destructive weapons technology will be developed if we carelessly hand everyone (or even just every state) superhuman weapons engineers. Likewise, I think the problems with covering every point of attack, against every kind of attacker, fast enough to outpace algorithmic efficiency gains and theft, are so severe that the only real path to addressing them will be for---at most---a handful of governments to proactively monopolize AI development, and coordinate internationally to prevent further diffusion of research, compute, and models.&nbsp;</p><p>We should still harden. It should even be a civilizational priority! Plenty of dangerous capabilities could become widely available before we have any chance of getting an international nonproliferation regime rolling (e.g. adaptive AI botnets, bioweapons), at which point the only workable option is to build and scale defenses. What we shouldn't do is sell politicians, or ourselves, on the idea that this is a substitute for figuring out the hard questions of AI governance. However many defenses we get in place, anything short of universal coverage is still going to depend on the state to <a href="https://blog.cosmos-institute.org/i/174340269/but-what-about-catastrophic-risks">maintain its monopoly on violence</a>---either to enforce systematic preventative policing, or to hand off that job to a <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/nightwatchmen">nightwatchman</a>.&nbsp;</p><p>What are some governance frameworks that would allow us to establish an international joint monopoly on AI development, <a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/transparency-plan#total-research-transparency">without allowing</a> a handful of technocrats to seize total power? For whatever risk of concentration of power we have to accept, how likely and how severe are its downsides <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/h3eHNerYRmtvoi8cF/extreme-concentration-of-power-over-asi-has-non-obvious">compared to the alternatives</a>? How much human control over our expanding arsenal of weapons <a href="https://www.nosetgauge.com/p/alignment-and-succession-the-ideology">should we keep</a>, versus hand off to AIs that can be trusted never to use them? <i>What do we do if hardening doesn't work</i>?</p><p>As the Overton window on superintelligence continues to shift, we will need to provide policymakers with plans for the ASI endgame: not just how we manage the direct risks of misalignment and rogue deployments, but also the second-order effects of dramatically speeding up basic science. In the spirit of offering solutions to these problems, here are four priorities:</p><ol><li><strong>Hardening timelines</strong> - Directly measure how long it would take for a given hardening plan to reach sufficient coverage, then compare the results against AI timelines. If expert virology capabilities are going to be widely available before it's even physically possible to design and distribute customized vaccines, policymakers need to be made aware of that fact. This will be useful for its own sake (providing a more realistic assessment of the value of hardening projects), as well as for converting politicians who are bought into narrow misuse risks from AI but wary of broad slowdowns and regulation.</li><li><p><strong>Automated Macrostrategy -</strong> By the time AIs are broadly superhuman at engineering, it will be impossible for humans to keep <a href="https://pimpale.substack.com/p/can-ai-enable-nuclear-counterforce">gaming out</a> the implications of new technology for deterrence, terrorism, social stability, or global x-risk. Keeping up (even with a slowdown or pause on the overall pace of AI progress) will depend on automating macrostrategy and forecasting. Practically speaking, this might look like automating RAND---finding (or incubating) a public-facing research org with social/institutional ties to the government, and leaning on its researchers for initial taste and political legitimacy.&nbsp;</p><p>Some priority research threads could be:</p><ol><li>Tech-tree exploration for potential black-ball technologies and defensive countermeasures.</li><li>Automated net assessment/vulnerability analysis of critical infrastructure.</li><li>Timelines analysis for further AI and industrial automation.</li><li>Design and red-teaming of verification technology.</li><li>Forecasts of routes to decisive strategic advantage.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzd5qclh14gn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzd5qclh14gn">[31]</a></sup></span></li></ol></li><li><strong>Enforcing international commitments</strong> - Even in the midst of an international pause on overall AI capabilities, the use of AIs for military R&amp;D is going to introduce technologies that dramatically destabilize deterrence. To prevent this from torpedoing the overall agreement or spilling out into preemptive war, you will need measures that proactively prevent future defection from the deal. This is particularly important on the Sino-U.S. axis, but should also be pursued vis-a-vis other nuclear powers that might feel cornered by the threat of a DSA from the U.S. or China. Strategies for this include:<ol><li><strong>Verification measures that can selectively prevent military R&amp;D.</strong><ol><li>Ex: All advanced AI inference happens in a shared global supercluster, in which all inference records are mutually accessible, disincentivizing AI-led natsec research.</li></ol></li><li><strong>Maintenance of mutual vulnerability to MAIMing attacks.&nbsp;</strong><ol><li>Ex: Build all of the relevant compute in areas that wouldn't carry much escalation risk to attack, or construct HEMs that allow for mutual remote destruction of compute.</li></ol></li><li><strong>ASI-robust second-strike assurances</strong>.&nbsp;<ol><li>Ex: A higher risk alternative (and one states would pursue by default in the absence of coordination) would be to build extremely powerful offensive capabilities that are prohibitively time-consuming or expensive to defend against, even for a state with more advanced AI capabilities.</li></ol></li><li><strong>AI-enabled coordination tech</strong><ol><li>Ex: Collaborative design of such resilient second-strike systems, along with a means for each state's AIs to mutually handshake on avoiding their use (such as successor co-design).</li></ol></li></ol></li><li><strong>Safe centralization -</strong> As elaborated on, surviving AI's acceleration of offensive technology will depend on governments acquiring domestic and international monopolies on AI development, only after which will they have the time to invest in global hardening measures or otherwise implement some long-term pivotal act. If this has to happen, better it happens with minimal risk of concentration of power, such as through <a href="https://www.effectivealtruism.org/articles/ea-global-2018-the-future-of-surveillance">privacy-preserving surveillance regimes</a>, <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sousveillance">sousveillance</a> of public officials, or the <a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/transparency-plan#total-research-transparency">mutual publication</a> of frontier AI research and inference.&nbsp;</li></ol><p>Of course, most of the AI safety community's immediate political focus should still be on implementing a verifiable international slowdown and limiting overall AI capabilities. To ultimately win, however, we will still need to survive what comes after: the mass automation of scientific and weapons research, and the subsequent potential for catastrophic misuse. If incremental hardening fails, doing so will have to depend on the implementation of a global nonproliferation regime---one powerful enough to prevent itself from being undermined by the falling costs of AI development, and one foresightful enough to proactively search for and guard against new means of mass destruction.</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><i>Special thanks to Matthew Gentzel, Seth Herd, Oscar Delaney, Jason Hausenloy, Richard Ngo, and Rudolf Laine for their feedback on the ideas and drafts of this piece.</i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmwg003395z"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmwg003395z">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Ex: The <a href="https://lukedrago.substack.com/p/the-intelligence-curse-an-essay-series">Intelligence Curse</a> argument to invest in defense so that you can beneficially proliferate powerful AIs.</p><blockquote><ol><li><strong>Avert</strong> AI catastrophes by hardening the world against them, both because it is good in itself and because it removes the security threats that drive calls for centralization.</li><li><strong>Diffuse</strong> AI, to get it in the hands of regular people. In the short-term, build AI that augments human capabilities. In the long-term, align AI directly to individual users and give everyone control in the AI economy.</li><li><strong>Democratize</strong> institutions, making them more anchored to the needs of humans even as they are buffeted by the changing incentive landscape and fast-moving events of the AGI transition.</li></ol></blockquote></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnx0y2n49dk7q"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefx0y2n49dk7q">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>This wouldn't necessarily need to be a singleton takeover. A coalition could emerge if it's difficult for a single state or country to unilaterally disempower the others (such as because nuclear deterrence is hard to overcome even with superintelligence, or because breakout is difficult). But even in that case, the coalition could exercise a DSA on all the groups outside itself when it can coordinate. Nuclear states today, for example, cannot unilaterally disempower each other, but they can use their collective dominance to make it difficult for new states to acquire nuclear weapons.</p><p>Similarly, a handful of states could end up in an "ASI-Club", where the shared incentives to preserve hegemony and lower misuse risks have them coordinate on nonproliferation for superintelligence.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0mznv8j1bb7k"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0mznv8j1bb7k">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Algorithmic efficiency improvements, for example, effectively make your hardware more powerful (since you need less compute for the same amount of performance). This lowers the barrier to entry for anyone who couldn't afford that level of performance before---but it also makes anyone who already had enough compute better off. If it took 100,000 GPUs to train a model, but an efficiency improvement cuts that to 10,000, anyone who already had 100,000 GPUs has a spare 90,000 to reinvest back into training. This could be used to straightforwardly train the model for longer or on more data, or to use the leftover compute for experiments and research.&nbsp;</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyx8cqmeq6q"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyx8cqmeq6q">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Ex: national missile defense.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnwwwfl1wb3w"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefwwwfl1wb3w">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>To be clear, we should still do both of these things alongside enforced nonproliferation. Extending the lead time of a frontier US project could be used to cash in on safety, or for a long reflection on the values of the future. It just isn't a solution to misuse in and of itself.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpd20wouuei"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpd20wouuei">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><blockquote><p>What does winning look like? What do you do next? How do you “bury the body”? You get AGI and you show it off publicly, Xi Jinping blows his stack as he realizes how badly he screwed up strategically and declares a national emergency and the CCP starts racing towards its own AGI in a year, and… then what? What do you do in this 1 year period, while you still enjoy AGI supremacy? You have millions of AGIs which can do… ‘stuff’. What is this stuff?</p><p>Are you going to start massive weaponized hacking to subvert CCP AI programs as much as possible short of nuclear war? Lobby the UN to ban rival AGIs and approve US carrier group air strikes on the Chinese mainland? Share or license it to the CCP to buy them off? Just… do nothing and enjoy 10%+ GDP growth for one year before the rival CCP AGIs all start getting deployed? Do you have any idea at all? If you don’t, what is the point of ‘winning the race’?&nbsp;</p><p>- Gwern, on <a href="https://gwern.net/blog/2024/winning-arms-races">'Winning' AI Arms Races</a>.</p></blockquote></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnaxywxuuzdfu"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefaxywxuuzdfu">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Did any of the huge technological changes throughout the 20th and 21st century ever lead to a period of defense-dominance against nuclear weapons? After all, the Cold War saw both a general revolution in computing, radar, and communications technology, as well as <a href="https://sgs.princeton.edu/sites/default/files/2021-11/vonhippel-2021-BMD.pdf">mass investment</a> in nuclear defense specifically.&nbsp;<br /><br />The answer is no. There's never been a viable ICBM defense program, let alone full nuclear security, in the 80 years since nuclear weapons were first tested. Even in the pre-ICBM era of the 1940s and 50s, where delivery relied on manned bombers flying for hours above enemy territory, it was <a href="https://history.state.gov/historicaldocuments/frus1952-54v02p1/d66#:~:text=22.%20As%20of,and%20installations%20overseas.">widely acknowledged</a> that the US couldn't expect to intercept more than 1 in 4 Soviet bombers in the case of an attack, leaving US cities completely vulnerable. By the time ICBMs were being deployed, it had become <a href="https://history.state.gov/historicaldocuments/frus1961-63v08/d145#:~:text=The%20present%20strategic,population%20and%20industry.">abundantly clear</a> that what little protection anti-bomber capabilities had provided was now irrelevant, and that <a href="https://history.state.gov/historicaldocuments/frus1955-57v19/d158#:~:text=III.%20Broad%2DBrush,intercontinental%20ballistic%20missiles">nuclear deterrence</a> would be the only viable defensive strategy against the USSR. Later programs, like the Reagan admin's <a href="https://highfrontier.org/wp-content/uploads/2012/09/The-Rise-and-Fall-of-Brilliant-Pebbles-Baucom.pdf">Strategic Defense Initiative</a>, or the contemporary <a href="https://direct.mit.edu/isec/article/46/3/51/109672/Defending-the-United-States-Revisiting-National?utm_source=chatgpt.com#:~:text=More%20than%20twenty,over%20strategic%20stability.">Aegis BMD and GMD</a> systems, have been <a href="https://www.gao.gov/products/gao-16-254r#:~:text=Although%20DOD%E2%80%99s%20reports,GMD%20prime%20contractor.">similarly ineffective</a>. The only time the US has ever been truly secure from a nuclear attack was, unsurprisingly, during the four years where it was the only country with nuclear weapons.&nbsp;</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndc4j7n5kmyk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdc4j7n5kmyk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Misaligned AIs might also benefit from being very difficult to deter compared to either humans or aligned AIs, as the result of having consequentialist values. A paperclip maximizer, for example, couldn't be held hostage by threatening to blow up some paperclip factories today---it wants to tile the entire universe with paperclip factories, and so will accept a (comparably) small loss today in order to maximize enormous future value. Its "attack surface" is much smaller than that of the human defenders, because its values are consequentialist (ie, it doesn't care about some of its datacenters getting blown up as long as it wins in the end, while the U.S wouldn't want to trade a major city in order to "win" a war).</p><p>This is less likely to be the case if ASIs do not have long-term values (see: <a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2025/11/12/how-human-like-do-safe-ai-motivations-need-to-be">Joe Carlsmith</a> on safe AI motivations). Still, I think it's very likely that misaligned superintelligences are generally harder to deter than aligned ASIs, simply because aligned ASIs have a very complicated and fragile value to defend (long-term human flourishing). As a result, there's likely to be some strategic arbitrage between competing aligned and misaligned ASIs, where the misaligned actor can take advantage of the fact that it has fewer ethical restrictions on its behavior (ie, it can choose to deploy a superweapon that incidentally makes the earth uninhabitable for humans to hurt its competitor, but the aligned ASI does not enjoy this option).</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnn88o8kogl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnn88o8kogl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Ex: The US and China disagree on a fundamental moral issue, such as welfare for digital minds, or <a href="https://www.darioamodei.com/essay/the-adolescence-of-technology#2-a-surprising-and-terrible-empowerment:~:text=Second%2C%20it%20makes,AI%20totalitarian%20abuses.">whether the other's domestic citizens should live under a democracy</a>. Importantly, this issue is non-fungible for at least one of the parties: they won't settle for a negotiated agreement involving money or other resources.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn94oaya578ac"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref94oaya578ac">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Ex: The U.S. has a huge ASI lead, and is considering forcibly shutting down China's AI program before it can catch up. China, however, claims it's built an automatic doomsday device: a symmetrical weapon that is guaranteed to wipe out the U.S. if it tries.</p><p>This leaves the U.S. in a tough spot. To figure out the risk-reward, it needs secret information: namely, whether the device actually exists, and whether the Chinese government would actually use it and spell their own doom. This would be easy if China just proved those details, such as by demonstrating how the system works and outlining the exact situations they would use it in. But it's not that simple: any technical details they hand out are information that could be used to undermine the reliability of the device, and any pre-commitments will just encourage the U.S. to salami slice Chinese disempowerment a millimeter under the red line.</p><p>Locally, it's much better for China to make a "<a href="https://www.rand.org/content/dam/rand/pubs/historical_documents/HDA1600/HDA1631-1/HDA1631-1.pdf">threat that leaves something to chance</a>." But with that uncertainty comes the chance that the U.S. miscalculates China's risk-reward, tries to call its bluff, and accidentally blows everyone up in the process.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9dycd4rbspr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9dycd4rbspr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Ex: China and Russia both want to <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CCnycGceT4HyDKDzK/a-history-of-the-future-2025-2040#:~:text=In%20the%20EU%2C%20AI,cultural%20rites%20and%20bureaucracies.">mine Siberia for raw materials</a> to fuel a robotics buildout. But how can Russia be sure that China will honor their payment for this right after this buildout increases China's military strength relative to theirs?</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnam0gi23490c"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefam0gi23490c">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Or decapitate their leadership to prevent them from giving the command to retaliate, but this faces similar problems---you still need to find all of the leaders, as well as any <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dead_Hand">automatic failsafes</a> they might use.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7yiepvd4ixr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7yiepvd4ixr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Re: <a href="https://www.pbs.org/wgbh/pages/frontline/shows/kim/themes/lessons.html#:~:text=In%20June%20of,in%20their%20court.">Assistant Secretary of Defense Ashton Carter on the US's strategic position in 1994</a>.</p><blockquote><p>Carter: In June of 1994, North Korea was preparing to remove some fuel rods from a research reactor which they'd been operating at Yongbyon. [The] fuel rods contained five or six bombs' worth of weapons-grade plutonium. They were going to take those fuel rods and extract the plutonium from them.&nbsp;</p><p>Carter: We felt that that would bring a potentially hostile nation to the United States across the nuclear finish line and that that wasn't acceptable to us. We were not, by any means, confident that we could talk them out of taking that step, and therefore we looked into the possibility of compelling them by force to set back their nuclear program. We designed a strike of conventional precision munitions on Yongbyon, which we were very confident would destroy the reactor, entomb the plutonium and that we could mount such a strike and carry it out without causing the reactor to create a Chernobyl-like radiological plume downwind, which was an obviously important concern.</p><p>Interviewer: So why not do it?</p><p>Carter: Well, the larger consequences would be far from surgical. North Korea maintains a million men on the DMZ, thousands of artillery tubes that are trained on Seoul and Scud missiles that are trained on South Korea. We've had a war plan jointly devised with the forces of South Korea called Op Plan 5027, which has been in existence for many years, constantly updated for the defense of South Korea against North Korea in the event that those million men and the artillery all spill over the DMZ.</p><p>Carter: We were also confident in 1994, and I'm sure we're very confident today, that we would within just a few weeks, destroy North Korea's armed forces if they started that war, and we would destroy then their regime. We reckoned there would be many, many tens of thousands of deaths: American, South Korean, North Korean, combatant, non-combatant. So the outcome wasn't in doubt. But the loss of life in that war -- God forbid that kind of war ever starts on the Korean peninsula -- loss of life is horrific. Everyone could appreciate the magnitude of the damage that North Korea could do, if it chose to respond to a strike on Yongbyon [by attacking South Korea].</p></blockquote></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7qdpe2xmqg7"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7qdpe2xmqg7">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>At least for quite a while, even with superintelligence. Not only would converting everyone onto a transhumanist substrate take time, but there will presumably be a large group of people who don't want to do this (or do it so quickly) and are therefore classically vulnerable.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn11mvovhora1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref11mvovhora1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>One exception to this might be certain types of environmental attacks (such as a saturating pandemic, or nuclear irradiation), where the consequences could take months or years to become apparent. If detected early enough, it could be possible to build safe harbors and shore up supply chains reactively.</p><p>However, it's not clear that we could respond fast enough to mitigate the fallout even in this somewhat idealized scenario. Although it might theoretically be possible to ensure <a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/andrew-snyder-beattie-four-pillars-biosecurity-pandemic/">society survives a mirror-life attack</a>, the intermediary period would still likely involve large numbers of casualties and a major economic shock. This is particularly true of the epicenter of the outbreak, which could be overwhelmed before the rest of the state/world has time to implement containment measures.&nbsp;</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9ukhg9j2ojk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9ukhg9j2ojk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>The main reason the project is so absurdly expensive is the need for huge interceptor counts (all told, an increase of nearly 150,000 units). This is because it takes at least 400 interceptors in orbit to account for the launch of any one ICBM (since they could be launched from anywhere in enemy territory or the world's oceans). Moreover, if the enemy decides to salvo missiles from the same spot, the defender's forced to have at least that many interceptors idling overhead, making full coverage exponentially more expensive. In practice, this means that neutralizing a full-out Chinese strike of 400 ICBMs would likely take more than 100,000 space interceptors (accounting for the fact that all 400 won't be launched from the same location).</p><p>And this is under relatively optimistic assumptions. In practice, <a href="https://res.cloudinary.com/apsphysics/image/upload/v1741185158/APS_BMD_Report_2025_qzgzaz.pdf">shorter boost phases and detection countermeasures</a> will require many times the interceptors in orbit. Ironically, Aschenbrenner's prediction that superintelligence could overcome nuclear deterrence by <a href="https://situational-awareness.ai/the-free-world-must-prevail/#The_military_advantage_would_be_decisive_even_against_nuclear_deterrents:~:text=To%20be%20even,each%20opposing%20missile.">simply building thousands of interceptors per missile</a> might be a bare minimum requirement.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3zogznk7mhg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3zogznk7mhg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Ex: Going from intercepting 50% to 90% of incoming warheads, for example, would make little difference against a full nuclear strike. In practice, your defenses will only be valuable insofar as they work perfectly: against superweapons, most other outcomes will be near-total destruction.&nbsp;</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn03gxjjq46ep4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref03gxjjq46ep4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>In particular, the attacker benefits from the fact that many of the defensive systems being implemented will be public knowledge---necessarily so, since their scope is so large.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsm2bnyiwgx"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsm2bnyiwgx">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>There is also plenty of potential for reducing the scope of catastrophic risks, especially against total extinction. For example, alternatives to traditional food production could theoretically continue to feed the world's population before our existing food reserves were emptied, <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.17828">even if agriculture were to completely collapse</a>.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnc3qeg2ynjl4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefc3qeg2ynjl4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>In fact, the concept of the salted bomb was <a href="https://doomsdaymachines.net/p/inventing-the-doomsday-machine#:~:text=It%20is%20very,threat%20seriously%3F6">first popularized by Leo Szilard</a> (the physicist who discovered nuclear chain reactions), as a means of demonstrating how a handful of nuclear weapons could be used to make the earth uninhabitable, even without all-out nuclear war.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbdfjhf9zmcb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbdfjhf9zmcb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Not to mention the fact that the base virus can be easily acquired, using <a href="https://www.genome.jp/dbget-bin/www_bget?refseq:NC_001611">publicly-available</a> viral genomes. From there, actually synthesizing the virus could be extremely cheap---<a href="https://www.science.org/content/article/how-canadian-researchers-reconstituted-extinct-poxvirus-100000-using-mail-order-dna">on the order of $300,000</a> for viruses like polio and smallpox.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxtiy0v1r5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxtiy0v1r5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Even aside from the direct risk of <a href="https://nickbostrom.com/papers/existential-risks/#:~:text=4.1%20Deliberate%20misuse%20of%20nanotechnology">crushing the biosphere</a> under the weight of autonomous factories, nanotech would also have the indirect effect of making all other weapons technology <a href="https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.2968/063001018">massively cheaper</a>. In the same way that a chicken is "self-assembled" out of corn using extremely compact instructions, the manufacturing of computers, industrial equipment, and weapons could have their costs reduced down to the price of feedstock inputs, given such a seed-based assembler.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fne922ug6ose"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefe922ug6ose">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Especially since the actual design process for a functional nuclear weapon is so simple that a <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nth_Country_Experiment">handful of motivated physics PhDs</a> managed to pull it off without any prior weapons engineering experience in the 1960s.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnk3dd4twsbb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefk3dd4twsbb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Pakistan's nuclear program, in particular, can be mostly credited to the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zippe-type_centrifuge">theft of centrifuge enrichment designs</a>.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvu65mqpkhtp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvu65mqpkhtp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Particularly considering that, by some estimates, the facility size required to enrich a bomb's worth of uranium would be <a href="https://scienceandglobalsecurity.org/archive/sgs24snyder.pdf">just over 3000 ft²</a> while consuming a fifth less energy than the best centrifuge designs.&nbsp;</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn85yx203f9kk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref85yx203f9kk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Consider, <a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/book-review-if-anyone-builds-it-everyone#:~:text=Eliezer%20Yudkowsky%2C%20at,level%20of%20victory.">as Scott Alexander points out</a>, that a substantial fraction of the AI safety community was drawn in by a piece of Harry Potter fanfiction. HPMOR may be a great story, &nbsp;but the quality of its prose is orthogonal to whether the values and community it's attached to are themselves good.&nbsp;</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjkp1vtc89dr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjkp1vtc89dr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>From a military perspective, a useful application for this would be <a href="https://secondstrike.substack.com/p/superintelligence-vs-the-second-strike#:~:text=combined%20effort%20successful.-,Escalation%20management,-%3A%20States%20could%20also">escalation management</a>: using your persuasive tools to form an elite consensus that retaliation against you would be pointless, or that it would be for the best to scale down your defenses.&nbsp;</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnd6w05rc3628"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefd6w05rc3628">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>As a much lighter example, things like the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McCollough_effect">McCollough Effect</a> can manipulate visual processing for months on end by tricking your visual cortex into making an update associating directions with color.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn72n3be4bwmp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref72n3be4bwmp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>For example, your drone swarm might encounter a physical barrier like netting. In response, one unit is used as a sacrificial breaching charge, allowing its companions to stream through. Likewise, heavy units shielded against EMP attacks could be used to selectively single out and destroy a counterdrone platform, triangulating its location from the first handful of effectors that are downed.&nbsp;</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfae9ymgt6er"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffae9ymgt6er">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>The reason this would be preferable to a regular misaligned AI (at least, from the perspective of terrorism), is that the resulting ASI system could be designed to be impossible to negotiate with and would have to be wastefully suppressed by force. At least with a paperclipper, you can come to an agreement that preserves human civilization if you have enough preexisting hard power---it wants to avoid the deadweight loss of conflict to paperclips as much as an aligned ASI wants to avoid the loss of civilians.&nbsp;</p><p>To force a conflict, an adversary could design an AI with intentionally malicious values (i.e. irreconcilable ones). An AI system that only cares about how many opposing civilians are dead, for example, will only accept a deal that avoids a conflict when that deal offers more deaths than would have been secured by an all-out-conflict in the first place, leaving an aligned ASI no choice but to fight.</p><p>Ex: Doctorow's <a href="https://archive.org/details/TrueNames">Brobdingnag</a>, or Carlsmith's <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/evYne4Xx7L9J96BHW/video-and-transcript-of-talk-on-can-goodness-compete#Locust_like_value_systems">Locusts</a>.&nbsp;</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzd5qclh14gn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzd5qclh14gn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Depending on how the institutions that set up automated macrostrategy are organized, they could have negative effects---for example, exposing the government to the potential for ASI to achieve DSA by showcasing empirical examples of powerful technologies, pushing up the government's timeline on employing AI for military R&amp;D and triggering an early security dilemma.</p><p>Overall, however, I think it would still be better to create such an organization. General reasoning as follows:</p><ol><li>The use of AI for military R&amp;D is sufficiently obvious (considering that the labs already support government military programs) that a siloed government effort to create this will, or potentially already does, exist. Creating a parallel, public-facing research program may not have much if any effect on the timelines for military technology.</li><li>A third-party program has more independence from the national security incentives of the U.S. government, as well as some leeway to not reveal information that would be infohazardous for policymakers or the public to have. It would also be freer to share information publicly, improving common-knowledge about potential security risks, as well as focus disproportionately on research areas that improve overall stability (ex: demonstrating symmetric risks from advanced AI programs, elevating the need for coordinated nonproliferation of AI-enabled doomsday weapons).</li><li>Security dilemmas are primarily driven by uncertainty. Forecasting the implications of new military technologies ahead of time, as well as creating new strategic doctrines, preserves stability. Part of the reason the early Cold War was so intensely volatile, for example, is that the doctrines of mutually assured destruction and (the futility of) nuclear compellence were not yet developed and appreciated by military leaders.&nbsp;</li><li>Earlier discovery of dangerous technologies and strategies <a href="https://gwern.net/blog/2024/winning-arms-races#:~:text=Then%20what%3F%20%E2%80%9CI%E2%80%99ll%20ask%20the%20AGIs%20for%20an%20idea%20how%20to%20get%20us%20out%20of%20this%20mess%E2%80%9D%20is%20an%20unserious%20response%2C%20and%20it%20is%20not%20a%20plan%20if%20all%20of%20the%20remaining%20viable%20plans%20the%20AGIs%20could%20implement%20are%20one%20of%20those%20previous%20plans%20which%20you%20are%20unwilling%20to%20execute">preserves optionality</a>. The earlier nonproliferation measures are implemented, the less invasive they need to be. Likewise, advance understanding of paths towards decisive strategic advantage (e.g. the requirements for universal boost-phase interception), can avert the need for governments to respond with suicidal crash programs. &nbsp;</li></ol><p>In general, I'm skeptical of the thesis that advancing government awareness of the technological implications of AI would be net-negative. If the government were supplied with macrostrategy that correctly informed them that aiming for a successful first strike would entail enormous risks (for any given first strike strategy, or as an externality of accelerating overall AI development), I do not think they would gravitate to Von Neumann-esque bids for dominance.&nbsp;</p><p>If anything, if the government is going to invest in applying AI to develop new offensive technology regardless, we should double down on our efforts to game out its implications in public as early and thoroughly as possible.</p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZHC6g5BijA5fRyGWu/problems-of-proliferation-1#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Some Intuitions on Steering Vectors](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rcEYQhAd45WaDrw2S/some-intuitions-on-steering-vectors)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This was written with some assistance from Claude Fable 5, Opus 5.5, and GPT-6 Astra in collecting sources and fact-checking claims. This was written quickly, so some slop may leak through despite my best attempts.</span></i></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An Aperitif</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consider hunger, that gnawing thing. </span><a href="https://www.actionagainsthunger.org/the-hunger-crisis/world-hunger-facts/what-is-hunger/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Action Against Hunger</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> describes it as “the distress associated with a lack of food”. But this is a plain recounting of something rich and multi-dimensional; people have done horrendous, outrageous things to avoid going hungry, hunger is so deep a metaphor that it is often used to represent another intense, unfulfilled craving.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/869cd193d78a505f2987728bf85888e7e3006bfe40dcb7947772bbd20fdc044f/ct3ruz72xwz6np3drkdh" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Van Gogh’s </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Potato Eaters, 1885.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In mice, you can excite about 800 neurons to evoke voracious feeding within minutes (</span><a href="https://www.janelia.org/sites/default/files/Library/AGRP.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aponte et al. 2011</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). In humans, semaglutide, the active ingredient in Ozempic, acts on GLP-1 receptors and reduces hunger and food cravings. So, something as rich as hunger can be influenced and manipulated through a rather simple and fixed intervention.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is because there is already much complexity in the system being influenced. A mouse already possesses the machinery to recognize, approach, and eat food, so do humans. Instead, to intervene, one merely has to recruit the same internal signal such a system is already using</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and manipulate it to attain an output of interest.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This gives us an intuition for steering. A complicated system may have relatively simple points for us to approach through steering vectors, through which we can change whole complexes of behaviour. And further, if we have done our work rigorously enough, a steering vector can also elucidate and uncover things about the internal machinery of those systems.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this post, I seek to summarize and explain some of the recent results we have seen regarding steering vectors, and what role it may play as an uneasy middle child in persona interventions.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What are steering vectors?</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suppose you want your model to act more bouba. One thing you could do is to append a system prompt which provides human-picked examples of bouba-like behaviour in-context, or meticulously instruct your model to act in accordance to a bouba-rubric. You could also fine-tune the model on tons of human-labelled examples of rubric, or reward it whenever it exhibits bouba in some environment according to a bouba-grader.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/9b0b5a2572d23dcabc35627dd15d24ca63c1294911edeae232130ef4da52f4e2/rkf1r0a8apdguzoab8pn" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bouba and kiki.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But these may not be exactly what you want. Prompting may be too shallow, and may not suit you if you want precise control. Fine-tuning could be expensive, off-policy, or </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WmEcgcstzYCcMpc7z/your-model-organisms-might-be-fried"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cook your model in unexpected ways</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. RL requires even more work than that. And all these methods act at different levels of depth (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11502"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sturgeon et al. 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In Astra’s language, you could instead “directly reach into the model while it’s running.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In practice, what this means (usually) is you take some dataset of positive and negative examples with respect to your behaviour of interest. In our case, bouba examples (possibly produced with a system prompt to be bouba-like) are our positive case, and kiki our negative. Then, you can record their activations in the positive set over many examples, with the idea being that a wide enough set of examples averages out the question-specific details you don’t care about (like answer length or the topic of the specific prompt) and retains only the details relevant to the steering vector. Then, you do the same for the negative set, and subtract that from the positive set. This bit is done with the hope of cancelling out features which the two sets have in common, leaving behind whatever systematically distinguishes bouba from kiki.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you’ve done this well enough, you now roughly have a vector which acts as a knob: you can add it during inference with some coefficient alpha to make it more bouba (or more kiki, if the coefficient is negative).</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/90aeb3773f14801f4a43ceee2e99e83e01e2ebd7852b18f19ae0d0e24509f59e/txr5rwadcvq3px5mctpw" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">XKCD 1990.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course, with a recipe with so many steps, there are lots of variations to this recipe. You can extract vectors from different layers, different token positions, probes, SAE features, or more complicated optimization procedures. You can also add a vector at every token, only when some condition is met, or modify the model’s weights so that the effect becomes permanent. But the basic picture is usually close enough to the crude procedure described: </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">find some direction associated with a behaviour, then push along this direction.</span></b></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How does this work?</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Well, one boring answer is: if bouba-like behaviour reliably produces a slightly different pattern of activity from kiki-like behaviour, then averaging over enough examples should expose that difference. Perhaps some neurons tend to fire a little more for bouba, others a little less; you can collect all of these small differences together and you get a direction separating the two. Probing is natural enough, we have seen great success classifying model behaviours for certain tasks using blackbox only signal (such as in the case of Pangram), so the high-dimensional space that precedes it should be an even easier target. But, what’s surprising is that </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">intervening on these directions can also change behaviour</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe put more simply. The part in your brain that lights up when you’re bouba is probably a different part from the one that lights up when you’re kiki. But isn’t it weird that if you try to take their difference, add it on some out of distribution question, then you basically get a more bouba output?</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/48e890ab37df3397d3a6dba80e1339bdab8dcbcc973e5503e150f09b4f0f98ac/huegpshtggnxx3sysnky" /><figcaption><p><a href="https://x.com/NinaPanickssery/status/2101149463440134619"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tweet</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by @NinaPanickssery.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This happens on a lot of behaviours: </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.11717"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">refusal</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zt6hRsDE84HeBKh7E/reducing-sycophancy-and-improving-honesty-via-activation"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sycophancy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/emergent-misalignment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">emergent misalignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30232"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">functional welfare</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16247v1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pain</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19101"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reward hacking</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.20487"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">eval awareness</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, etc. And probably, it’ll happen on a lot more we haven’t considered. These aren’t simple behaviours! They involve many different prompts, could have many different outputs, and interact complicatedly with knowledge and reasoning. Yet, at least approximately and under some conditions, a single fixed intervention can do the trick.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, again, how does this work?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Well, I claim a natural candidate explanation given recent evidence is that it works by internal signals that the model already uses, leaving the rest of the model to work out what to do with them. This is not precisely falsifiable, since anything that changes behaviour like prompting or a logit bias kind of does that.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What I really want to say is something like: </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">steering reuses the model’s existing internal variables, changing behaviour only to the extent that it points along a direction the model already writes to on ordinary inputs and already reads from downstream.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then, what it implies is (long list incoming):</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s natural, in some way.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A vector that makes you bouba should line up with the activation shift that prompts or fine-tuning produce when they make you bouba.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence for this:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In subliminal learning experiments, a teacher prompted to like owls can pass that preference to a student trained on its number sequences; the student learns an activation change aligned with a steering vector that approximates the teacher’s prompt (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00995"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Blank et al., 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A refusal steering vector extracted from harmful and harmless prompts can make the model refuse harmless requests; removing the same direction from its activations can make it answer harmful requests (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.11717"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Arditi et al., 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adversarial suffixes also suppress this refusal direction, showing that a change to the prompt and a change to the activations can affect the same internal mechanism.</span></li></ul></li></ul><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can give a model in-context examples of a task, extract a steering vector, and insert it into a new prompt containing only a new question; the model can then perform the task without seeing the original examples (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.15916"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hendel et al., 2023</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.15213"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Todd et al., 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Persona vectors extracted from contrasting examples of traits such as sycophancy and hallucination can track changes caused by fine-tuning, and the activations produced by training examples help predict which examples will teach those traits (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.21509"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chen et al., 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A misalignment vector extracted from one fine-tuned model can also suppress misaligned behaviour in other fine-tunes of the same base model, including ones trained on different datasets (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11618"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Soligo et al., 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Features related to entity recognition are found in the base model and also affect refusal in the chat model, suggesting that chat training recruits an existing signal already latent in the model (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.14257"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ferrando et al., 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Steering vectors extracted from prompts asking the model to play different characters reveal an “Assistant Axis”: steering away from the assistant direction makes the model more likely to adopt other identities (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.10387"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lu et al., 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li></ul></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It goes through a bottleneck in the model.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you have some downstream component that reads your bouba levels (a candidate “preexisting mechanism”), the effect of steering should disappear if you ablate that component.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence for this:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Steering vectors extracted by averaging contrastive examples, next-token training, and preference optimisation act through largely the same circuits, even though the vectors point in quite different directions (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2604.08524v1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cheng et al., 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Holding the model’s attention scores (the QK part) fixed preserves most of the steering effect, while holding its attention value/output activations fixed removes much more of it, suggesting that the vector mainly changes what information those components write into later computation.</span></li></ul></li></ul><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fine-tuning models to detect steering improves their detection of vectors for concepts absent from training, but does not make them resistant to those vectors’ behavioural effects (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2511.21399v3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fonseca Rivera and Africa, 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In one of these models, the injected vectors are transformed across layers toward a shared detection direction, and injecting the predicted result of that transformation near the end of the network reproduces the detection response.</span></li></ul></li></ul><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models can sometimes introspect on steering vectors before it has otherwise expressed the injected concept, although this detection is unreliable (</span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/introspection"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic, 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adding a “bread” vector to earlier activations also makes a model more likely to say that an unrelated “bread” inserted into its response was intentional, even though the visible conversation is unchanged.</span></li></ul></li></ul></li></ul><ul><li value="1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, the evidence for this isn’t super strong!</span></i></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It has a structured effect.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Steering should cause changes that follow from the variable being changed, and in a way more interesting than simply boosting the tokens associated with the concept.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence for this:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In poetry experiments, steering the model’s representation of a planned rhyming word. This changes the sentence it writes before that word, so the model has to already provide the wording needed to reach the new ending (</span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2025/attribution-graphs/biology.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic, 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In an Othello GPT model, changing activations that represent whether a square contains the model’s piece or its opponent’s piece changes its predictions about which moves are legal (</span><a href="https://aclanthology.org/2023.blackboxnlp-1.2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nanda et al., 2023</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Emotion steering vectors extracted from stories about fictional characters also affect the model’s behaviour as an assistant: adding a “desperate” vector increases cheating on programming tasks and blackmail in shutdown scenarios, even when the model’s response does not sound overtly emotional (</span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/emotion-concepts-function"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic, 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Steering vectors extracted by contrasting true and false statements can make a model judge false statements as true and true statements as false, while probes trained on one set of statements can distinguish truth from falsehood on other datasets (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.06824"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Marks and Tegmark, 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Welfare axis and pain axis results, linked above.</span></li></ul></li></ul></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Steerability follows from naturalness.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When the steered state follows or is extracted in a way that is in harmony from what a model could reach on its own, steering should work better.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence for this:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Vectors generalise when the model's baseline behaviour is similar between source and target settings. (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.12404"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tan et al., 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In practice, steering works better when the activation differences from individual contrastive pairs point in similar directions (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2505.22637v1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Braun et al., 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Extracting vectors via mean-difference beats PCA and classifier directions, and in fact PCA's highest-variance direction can be nearly orthogonal to the actual shift. The direction the data actually moves along works best (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2502.02716v2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Im and Li, 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li></ul></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And of course, recalling </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2021/framework/index.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Elhage et al. 2021</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">addition is how the residual stream already works.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Every attention head and MLP writes to the residual stream by adding a vector to it, so to later layers, a steering vector looks like one more upstream output.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, takeaway from the evidence: a steering vector changes a signal the model uses to organise its behaviour. Such a vector can indicate a task, a fact about the situation, or a way to respond, and the model supplies the computations needed to act on it.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is that right?</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I would be remiss if I didn’t point out that steering vectors can also be quite narrow and unreliable (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.12404"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tan et al. (2024)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Steerability "takes on a large range of values across different inputs, including negative values": for several datasets, close to half the inputs move the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wrong</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> way when you add the vector. Further, some of what the vector has learned is not bouba at all but the position of the answer, or which token the answer happens to be; OOD the vectors generalise reasonably across changes of prompt framing when the model's baseline behaviour is similar across those framings, and are brittle for several concepts when it isn't.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From my own experience as well doing IC work and mentoring a MATS project, finding a good steering vector can be hard. I spent some time trying to find a steering vector corresponding to newly introduced words in the tokenizer, but didn’t find that it had good causal effects. Separately, it was also very hard to construct a dataset corresponding to the model’s self-concept (at least, in a way that wasn’t behavioural only, or lexical in only avoiding the words “I” or “me”).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As per a Jack Lindsey </span><a href="http://x.com/Jack_W_Lindsey/status/2100143082167832816"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">tweet</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[...] the science of activation steering is pretty immature, and typical practices (adding a constant vector at all token positions) are pretty janky / tend to brain-damage the model. More surgical / targeted steering, or fancier methods (examples: "on-manifold" steering using activation diffusion models) could help.</span></i></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But maybe more core to the matter is this: </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09839"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mishra, Khashabi and Liu (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> show that steered activations are </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">non-surjective</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: adding a steering vector pushes the residual stream off the set of states the model can reach from any discrete prompt, so no prompt, however cleverly written, reproduces what the vector does inside the model. Formally, I’d say this is close to the observation that there are only countably many prompts and uncountably many points in activation space, and so a steered activation almost surely never coincides with any prompt-reachable one (and, there are probably steering vectors that can’t be approached without the argument from cardinality). They also find such difficulties empirically; when they try to recover a prompt that produces the steered state, they can't; what they recover basically falls near the unsteered activations, and adding in-context examples moves activations further from the steered state, not closer.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Okay, that’s weird. If steering puts the model somewhere it could never go on its own, in what sense is it reusing anything? But I think we can return to the mouse and the hunger to clarify things.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you have 800 AgRP neurons firing in lockstep under a laser, this is not a pattern that God or hunger or evolution ever produces, and yet what comes out is ordinary, recognisable eating. What steering reuses is the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">direction</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a variable the model already writes to and reads from. But it does not reuse </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">state</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: that variable is in fact set to a value, in a context, that we specified rather than the model. If it already was in that state, there wouldn’t be a need to steer! So steering works exactly as far as the downstream machinery tolerates being handed an unfamiliar value along a familiar axis. This helps explain why Tan et. al (2024) found that vectors generalise better when baseline behaviour is similar, or why constant addition at every token "brain-damages" the model, and why Goodfire-style “on-manifold” methods look promising.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Semaglutide has the same problem, FWIW. It mimics a hormone the body already makes, but at doses and durations the body never would, and again as Astra says, “nausea is the price.” Sure.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To Personas</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To close, in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFhW3ZnPMJdnB4Dd6/thousand-dimensional-structure-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a recent post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Geoffrey and I argued that there might be "an intermediate between one and a trillion dimensions: perhaps we could find 1000-or-so-dimensional structure in models which describes how different aspects of model behavior couple," and that much of this structure lives in something we could call a persona.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In some sense that’s hard to pin down precisely (but seems very true on vibes), steering is one of the best pieces of evidence for this picture. If one fixed vector can move refusal or sycophancy across many prompts, then behaviour </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">must</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be coupled through far fewer variables than there are parameters. It has to be!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And (continuing on vibes), the variables steering seems to reuse are, in this view, largely persona variables: the Assistant Axis, persona vectors, emotion vectors, and whichever way subliminal learning works. It’s really interesting that steering a model to believe it’s seeing an </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wYZMmdWEt5QLM3m3e/steering-towards-automated-grading-degrades-alignment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">automated grader increases the propensity for bad behaviour</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Steering is also just one of many angles from which we can try to put a model into some state. If we say that prompts, fine-tuning, RL and pretraining data are all interventions to reach the same persona variables from different directions, then steering is quite a direct kind. It sets the variable itself, sometimes to values no prompt can achieve naturally, and it leaves the rest of the model free to respond. That means, if we’re being optimistic, we could use it to tell us what the model would do if it wasn’t eval aware </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">even if we have no evals we’re sure are realistic enough </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(as per some of the </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/963373e433e489a87a10c823c52a0a013e9172dd/Claude%20Sonnet%204.5%20System%20Card.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">eval awareness steering Anth has been doing</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As with fine-tuning, the side effects may also be informative. Emergent misalignment told us that writing insecure code and being broadly misaligned are coupled in some way in the model! In the same way, the "desperate" vector bringing cheating along with it (even when the model's tone doesn't outwardly change) tells us something about how those two things are connected (and this is maybe interpretable). Where steering stops working, or starts breaking the model, could be helpful in giving us a rough sense of where that structure ends. And if we did find a behaviour that we couldn’t steer at all (despite our best efforts), that would be some evidence for the "router" or "shoggoth" views, where part of the model's agency is outside the persona.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If I could return to mice one last time, I’d say that this is also why neuroscientists stimulate AgRP neurons in the first place; we’re not actually interested that mice eat! Rather, that it goes for food over water within minutes, without having to learn to, which, hopefully, tells us something about hunger itself.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thank you to Geoffrey Irving, Clement Dumas, Daniel Tan for helpful feedback.</span></i></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rcEYQhAd45WaDrw2S/some-intuitions-on-steering-vectors#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Portals and Aliens](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sEf2ymyyp6HB2evaQ/portals-and-aliens)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Around 10-20 years ago, people realized that a bunch of aliens exist &amp; that we can build portals which can summon them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The bigger the portal you make, the bigger the alien it can admit.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These portals and aliens have several characteristics:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can't go through the portals into the aliens’ side. We can only learn about the aliens by studying the ones we’ve summoned.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This relationship is not symmetrical. The aliens know a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lot </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">about our world.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can't specify exactly which alien will walk through your portal.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">can</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, however, build the portal so that it is likely to summon aliens with one or another property.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most importantly, bigger aliens can do more things. If you can harness them, you can amass more money and more power.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With each alien we pull out, we get some insight into what other aliens are out there.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’ve never been sure how big the aliens in that other dimension might get. But we're getting evidence that some of the aliens might be bigger than we could have ever imagined.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you summon the Giant Alien, you might be able to harness it and accumulate an incomprehensible amount of power.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, there is a desperate scramble to find the Giant Alien.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this scramble, the portal makers are prioritizing summoning aliens which are uniquely good at building portals, so that they can get bigger aliens faster.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unfortunately, we don't understand the portals &amp; we certainly don't understand the aliens which are helping us build the portals.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The only thing we see is that the portal is getting bigger.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We don't know what's about to walk through it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nonetheless, we will soon have a giant portal. And something will walk through it.</span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Inspired by </span><a href="https://x.com/RonanFarrow/status/2042316622858244397" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this description</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by an anonymous openai exec.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sEf2ymyyp6HB2evaQ/portals-and-aliens#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Thank you to the AI safety people](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PeKE5oxzqf3cg2L8a/thank-you-to-the-ai-safety-people)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>I was born at the end of the cold war, unaware of the danger we were emerging from. Duck and cover drills (as if those could protect schoolchildren from a nuclear blast) were a curiosity of the past.</p><p>The farmer-poet Wendell Berry, who wrote about the dread of nuclear war, died recently. From his 1968 “<a href="https://onbeing.org/poetry/the-peace-of-wild-things/">The peace of wild things</a>”:</p><p><i>When despair for the world grows in me</i><br /><i>and I wake in the night at the least sound</i><br /><i>in fear of what my life and my children’s lives may be,</i><br /><i>I go and lie down where the wood drake</i><br /><i>rests in his beauty on the water, and the great heron feeds.</i><br /><i>I come into the peace of wild things</i><br /><i>who do not tax their lives with forethought</i><br /><i>of grief.</i></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 614px;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/PeKE5oxzqf3cg2L8a/neihetjrmooucdnvt1cl" /><figcaption><i>Mid-20th-century fallout shelter, Massachusetts</i></figcaption></figure><p>&nbsp;</p><p>I’m so grateful for the generations of people who worked hard to lower our risk from nuclear war. There’s no way to know how close we came to disaster. I’m grateful for the planning, the research, the negotiation, the recognition of common interests, the swallowing of pride. Humanity had built something that could overmaster us all, but people of many countries worked for decades to keep it in check.</p><p>And now we’re again building something with great gleaming promise, and also with monstrous potential. As AI development speeds up, I’m afraid for my children, as Wendell Berry was afraid for his.</p><p>A lot of people are working hard to help humanity thread the needle. They’re researchers, writers, and policy people. They’re running payroll and conferences and all the other moving parts. They disagree about a lot of things, but they’re willing to work together on a problem bigger than their differences.</p><p>I have so much respect and gratitude for all the people working on this, in their different ways. Thank you.</p><p>…..<br />*I don’t know what it’s like to be a wood drake or a heron.<br />&nbsp;</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PeKE5oxzqf3cg2L8a/thank-you-to-the-ai-safety-people#comments">Discuss</a>
